Let me think about the proof strategy.

We have a locally nameless STLC with CBV. We need to prove strong normalization.

Standard approach: Logical relation / reducibility candidates for CBV STLC.

For CBV, the simplest is to use a "hereditarily strongly normalizing" / reducibility predicate. But there's a well-known trick: define the logical relation as "SN plus step-closure" for base type, and for arrow type: t is SN and for all u in R(T1), step-closure etc.

Actually there's a nice approach for CBV strong normalization: define

- R(Bool) = SN (strongly_normalizing)
- R(T1 -> T2) = { t | SN t /\ forall u, R(T1) u -> R(T2) (t u) }

Wait but for CBV, applications reduce the argument before substituting. Hmm. Actually a cleaner standard trick: define the logical relation with "expansion" (step closure) at each level:

IR(T) = set of terms t such that SN t and for all t', t --> t' -> IR(T) t'.

This is the "hereditarily terminating" definition. For Base: SN. For arrow: t in IR(T1->T2) iff SN t and for all t', t --> t' -> IR(T1->T2) t', and for all v, value v -> IR(T1) v -> IR(T2) (t v). Hmm, actually one can do:

Define `R T t` as:
- R Bool t := SN t
- R (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ (forall t', step t t' -> R (T1->T2) t') /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> ... )

Hmm, this is coinductive-ish and hard in Coq. But actually, since we have SN as an inductive, we can define it by well-founded recursion... Alternatively, use the standard "CR" approach.

Let me recall the standard result: For CBV STLC, the simplest SN proof is via the "reducibility" definition where:

- R(Bool) = { t | SN t }
- R(T1 -> T2) = { t | SN t /\ forall u, R(T1) u -> R(T2) (t u) }

Then fundamental theorem: Gamma |- t : T with Gamma substituted by values in R(T_i) implies R(T)(t).

But the issue for CBV: the application rule. In CBV, `t1 t2` steps to `t1' t2` when t1 steps, and to `v1 t2'` when t2 steps (with v1 a value), and beta when both are values. To show R(T2)(t1 t2) given R(T1->T2)(t1) and R(T1)(t2), we need to show SN(t1 t2). Given SN(t1), SN(t2), and for all v-value in R(T1) ... hmm. The standard argument: prove SN of application by induction on SN(t1), and nested induction on SN(t2). When t1 is a value (abs) and t2 is a value in R(T1), then t1 t2 in R(T2) by definition (if t1 is in R(T1->T2) meaning it's SN and all applications to R-args are R). But if t1 is an abstraction, then it's a value and the third component gives that. If t1 is not a value... hmm, but the SN hypothesis on t1: any t1' with t1 --> t1' is SN. But R(T1->T2) needs to be preserved by steps: that's the "backward closed"/"CR" property.

Hmm, that's where the definition gets tricky. With R(T1->T2) = SN(t) /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u), the step-closure of R doesn't follow directly — but actually it's a standard lemma: if t in R(T1->T2) and t --> t', then t' in R(T1->T2), because... you need to show SN(t') (yes, from SN(t)) and forall u in R(T1), R(T2)(t' u). Hmm, we know R(T2)(t u) and SN(t1 u). t' u is a step of t u (via ST_App1, requires locally_closed u). So we need: if SN(x) and x --> x', then... no, R(T2)(x) does not imply R(T2)(x') unless R is closed under forward steps. Hmm, but actually SN is closed under forward steps, and we need R(T2)(t' u) from R(T2)(t u) — that's the wrong direction: t u --> t' u, so we need R to be "backward closed" under reverse steps. Hmm, we need R(T2)(t' u) given t u --> t' u and R(T2)(t u). That is: R is closed under inverse of step? That's not true in general for SN-based definitions (SN(x) and x --> y gives SN(y), forward direction).

Wait — but we need the reverse: from R(t u) with t u --> t' u deduce R(t' u). That's false: take t u = (lambda x. x) true with R... hmm at base type R = SN, and if x --> y and SN x, then SN y. Actually the direction is: x --> y, SN(x) => SN(y). That's forward. Here we have t u --> t' u, and R(t u), want R(t' u). For base type that's SN(t u) -> SN(t' u), which is true! Because SN(x) and x --> x' gives SN(x'). Great, so for base type R is forward-closed. For arrow type, inductively, R is forward-closed too — can prove by induction on types? Let's see: R(T1->T2)(t) = SN(t) /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u). If t --> t' and R(t), then SN(t') and for u in R T1, R T2 (t u) and t u --> t' u (by ST_App1, needs lc u), so by IH-forward-closure (R T2 forward closed), R T2 (t' u). But wait, this is mutual: forward closure of R(T) for all T, proved by induction on T, requires forward closure of R T2 at smaller type. Yes, structural induction on T works: R(T1->T2) uses R T2's forward-closure, which is at a smaller type. And R T1's forward closure isn't needed. Good.

So define:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => SN t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Hmm, but actually with this definition, is R(T1->T2) closed under forward steps? Yes as argued above. But actually we might not even need forward-closure in the R-to-R direction... Let me think about what's needed in the fundamental theorem.

Actually, hold on. The key difficulty: showing SN(t1 t2) from R(T1->T2)(t1) and R(T1)(t2). We need a general lemma: if SN(t1), SN(t2) and t1 is a value (abs) implies ... hmm.

Let me think. Standard approach for CBV: prove "if SN(t1), SN(t2), and (t1 value -> R T2 (t1 t2))... " hmm.

Alternative: define R with the "step-closed" clause built in, à la Girard's reducibility candidates:

R(Bool) = SN
R(T1->T2) = { t | SN t /\ forall t', t --> t' -> R(T1->T2) t' /\ forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (t v) }

That's a coinductive-ish negative definition — problematic in Coq (this is a "closure" definition requiring recursion on the relation, but it's an inductively-defined-type with negative occurrence... actually it's just a Fixpoint returning a function into Prop, but `t --> t' -> R (T1->T2) t'` refers to R at the same type, which is not structurally smaller → rejected).

The standard trick to handle this in Coq: use a well-founded recursion on SN or define R by recursion on the "reduction" with accessibility. Alternatively, use the "acyclic" approach: define R in terms of SN plus a further condition, which is what I did first.

Let me reconsider approach 1 and figure out how to prove SN of application.

Definition:
- R Bool t := SN t
- R (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u).

Lemmas needed:

1. R T t -> SN t  (projection; for base it's identity).

2. Forward closure: R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'. Induction on T. For arrow: SN t' from SN t. For u in R T1: R T2 (t u), and t u --> t' u via ST_App1 with lc u — need locally_closed u. Hmm! Is R T u implying locally_closed u? In CBV STLC, all terms of the relation come from typed terms, so they're locally closed. But for the general lemma we need `locally_closed u`. Hmm.

Note ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2` where t2 is the argument. That's a well-formedness condition on the reduction relation. So forward closure at arrow type requires the argument be locally closed. We could either prove R T u -> locally_closed u (true for well-typed terms, but the logical relation itself doesn't guarantee it: e.g., R T (tm_bvar 0)? SN of a bvar is trivially true, and for arrow type... hmm, for T=Bool, R Bool (tm_bvar 0) = SN (tm_bvar 0) which holds. So R doesn't imply lc.)

Option: strengthen R to include locally_closed: R T t := SN t /\ locally_closed t /\ (for arrow, forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u)). Hmm, is forward closure then provable? For arrow: need lc t', which follows from lc t and t --> t'? Is step closed under lc? Probably yes: step preserves locally-closedness. Actually we can prove: t --> t' -> locally_closed t -> locally_closed t'. Hmm, for ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T body) v, lc t means lc (abs T body) i.e. lc_at 1 body? Wait lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t1) requires lc_at 1 t1. And open t1 v: we need lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v t1). Given lc_at 1 t1 and lc_at 0 v. That's a standard lemma (open preserves closure). Fine.

Alternatively, avoid needing forward closure entirely?

Let me think about what the fundamental theorem needs.

Fundamental theorem: for context Gamma and substitution sigma (mapping atoms to terms) with forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x), we have Gamma |- t : T -> R T (subst sigma t).

For T_App: t = t1 t2, t1 : T1 -> T2, t2 : T1. IH gives R (T1->T2) t1s and R T1 t2s. Unfolding R at arrow: SN t1s and forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t1s u). Set u := t2s → R T2 (t1s t2s). Done — no forward closure needed here!

But for T_Abs: t = abs T1 t1, type T1 -> T2, need R (T1->T2) (abs T1 t1s):
- SN (abs ...) — an abstraction is a value, presumably SN since it can't step? Well, lc matters: an abs steps only via ST_AppAbs when applied, not by itself. Actually ST_AppAbs is about app; an abs alone doesn't step. So SN(abs) always? We need "forall t', abs --> t' -> SN t'", and there are no steps from abs. But the step relation on an abs — indeed none of the constructors produce `tm_abs --> _`. So SN(abs T body) for any body. Good (trivial).
- forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 ((abs T1 t1s) u). Hmm! This requires showing R T2 of an application where the function is an abs and the argument u is in R T1. We know R T1 u doesn't give us lc u necessarily. The beta step ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed (abs T1 t1s) and value u. Hmm, but the result should be a substitution instance: (abs T1 t1s) u --> open t1s u if lc(abs) and value u.

Hmm, this is where the standard "R-closed under beta with R-arguments" lemma is needed: if R T1 u and (u is a value or something), then R T2 (open t1s u) given the context extension. Actually in the fundamental theorem for abs, we typically prove directly:

forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u) requires knowing R T2 (open t1 u) and then app reduces to it... but app (abs) u doesn't reduce to open t1 u unless u is a value and abs is lc.

So the fundamental theorem needs the "CR" properties: R T is closed under the head-expansion: if t --> t' and R T t', then R T t? That's backward closure, which for a "reducibility" relation means: if all reducts of t are in R, then t is in R... Not true for SN alone (SN t' doesn't give SN t, since t might loop... no wait, t --> t', SN t' but t may have other reducts; but for a deterministic relation? CBV STLC step is deterministic, but SN requires all paths finite; with t --> t' and SN t', t' has no infinite path but other reducts of t might loop).

Hmm. So backward closure isn't available. The standard way: in the fundamental theorem, prove a stronger statement for the arrow case: show R T2 (t1 t2) directly by showing SN(t1 t2) and the forall-u condition.

Alternative approach that works well for CBV: use the "substitution/term interpretation" with a logical relation defined with SN, and prove that "R T t" implies SN of the application using a lemma about application:

Lemma app_SN : forall t1 t2, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> R (T1->T2) t1 -> R T1 t2 -> R T2 (app t1 t2).

Proof: unfold R at T2. Hmm, need for both base and arrow cases.

For T2 = Bool: need SN (app t1 t2). Standard: induct on SN t1, then on SN t2. Cases:
- t1 not a value: any step of app t1 t2 is either app t1' t2 (if t1 --> t1', needs lc t2) or, if t1 is a value... But t1 not a value excludes ST_AppAbs and ST_App2? Hmm, ST_App2 requires value t1. So if t1 is not a value, only ST_App1 applies: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 with lc t2. Then by IH (since t1' is a reduct of t1, SN t1'), app t1' t2 is SN. So need SN t1'... obtained from SN t1. And need lc t2 for the step — we get that from... hmm, need to know lc t2. If R T1 t2 doesn't give lc, we're stuck. Unless: we case on whether the step actually occurred... but we must handle all steps t' with app t1 t2 --> t'. For ST_App1 we need lc t2 as a premise, which we don't have from R T1 t2.

So: strengthen R to include locally_closed. Let's do:

R T t := SN t /\ locally_closed t /\ (match T with Bool => True | Arrow T1 T2 => forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (t u) end)%type

Hmm, but then in the abs case we need lc (abs T1 t1s) which comes from the typing (locally closed) — fine.

Now for app_SN: t1 in R(T1->T2) gives SN t1, lc t1, and forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t1 u). Take u = t2: gives R T2 (app t1 t2). Wait!!! That's exactly what we want! So no need for the nested induction at all — unfold R at arrow directly gives R T2 (app t1 t2).

Hmm, wait. Do we really need lc in R? Let's re-examine where lc is needed:

For non-value u, only app(abs, u') steps, and by induction on SN(u), those are handled by the third clause applied recursively: app(abs, u') in R T2 where R T2 needs a nested induction.

Hmm, this is the standard issue. Let me recall the standard CBV SN proof. The typical approach (e.g., from Pierce's Software Foundations, StlcStrongNorm for CBV? Actually SF's strong norm is for STLC with fixpoint?).

Alternatively, a cleaner approach for CBV: define the logical relation using "value interpretation" plus "term closure":

Define the logical relation on **terms** as:
- t ∈ R(T) iff SN(t) and (t is a value → t ∈ V(T))? Hmm.

Standard "Tait-style" for CBV:
- V(Bool) = {true, false} (values that are SN trivially)
- V(T1->T2) = { abs T1 body | forall u ∈ R(T1), open body u ∈ R(T2) }
- R(T) = { t | SN(t) and for all v, t -->* v with v a value implies v ∈ V(T) }

Hmm, or more simply: R(T) = terms that are SN and whose value-normal-forms are in V(T). But with SN, every term reduces to a normal form which is a value? Hmm, in CBV STLC normal forms are values: if a term is normal, it's either a value or a stuck term (bvar/fvar-headed application or if with non-value scrutinee). Well-typed closed terms aren't stuck.

**Alternative approach for CBV SN via the "step-closure" definition using inductive-recursive or well-founded recursion.**

Let me think about what's actually needed and consider using a well-founded recursion based on `SN`.

Actually, there's a neat trick: define R T t as: SN t /\ for all t', multi step t t' -> normal-form-ish condition. I.e.:

R(T) := { t | SN t /\ forall t', t -->* t' -> (value t' -> V(T)(t')) } where for base type V(Bool) := everything? Hmm.

Actually, the cleanest for CBV: 

R(Bool) t := SN t
R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R(T1) u -> R(T2) (t u)

and prove everything with a general lemma using nested induction on SN derivations. Let me carefully reconsider the abs case:

Goal: R(T1->T2)(abs T1 b). Need:

(b) forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u).

So let's define:

R(Bool) t := SN t /\ lc t

Then:

Hmm.
    
Hmm, wait. But the `open b u` term: b is the substituted body `subst sigma t1`. When u is a value in R T1, and we know from the typing IH applied to an extended substitution... 

The standard structure: the fundamental theorem is proved w.r.t. an arbitrary substitution sigma. In the T_Abs case, we need to show for all u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u). For value u, app reduces to open (subst sigma t1) u = subst (sigma, x := u) (open t1 x)... And the IH (from T_Abs's premise) is: for all x not in L, R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 x)) where sigma' = (x := fvar x) :: sigma ≈ extended. So for value u ∈ R T1, we need a substitution lemma / "the interpretation depends only on R-related values": if sigma' = sigma with x := u, then subst sigma' (open t1 x) = open (subst sigma t1) u, and R T2 of it. This requires the IH to hold with u substituted, which requires a lemma that the fundamental theorem's statement is "substitution-closed" — i.e., the semantic interpretation is stable under substitution of R-related terms. Standard: prove the fundamental theorem with respect to a substitution and then a "substitution lemma for the logical relation": if for all x, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x) and (forall x, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma' x)) and sigma ≈ sigma' pointwise R-equal, then R T (subst sigma t) iff R T (subst sigma' t). Since the relation is defined syntactically, and substitution is syntax-directed, we'd prove that by induction on t: substituting sigma or sigma' gives... hmm, they're different terms, so no.

Standard approach in locally nameless (like SF's STLC_StrongNorm? Actually SF's "Stlc" chapter has normalization? Hmm). Let me recall the standard locally-nameless SN proof structure (e.g., from Engineering Formal Metatheory / Charguéraud's tutorial, or the "norm" chapter).

Let me consider a cleaner strategy: **use a substitution-based semantics** to simplify? No, we must use the supplied step relation.

OK here's another thought. The problem is CBV and the need for beta-expansion. Many CBV SN proofs use the trick: define R with SN and step-closure: R(T) t := SN t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> R(T) t') /\ (forall v, value v -> ???). Hmm.

Actually, let me look at this from a different angle. Consider the "reducibility candidates" for CBV from Girard's book / the standard "CR" for call-by-value (e.g., in "A Semantical Proof of Strong Normalization for Call-by-Value"). The standard definition:

A term t is in R(T) iff t is SN and for any t' such that t -->* t' with t' a value... hmm.

Alternative known simpler approach: **Define R via "all multi-step reducts are SN-able and value-typed"**:

R(Bool) t := SN t
R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V(T1->T2) v
where V(T1->T2)(abs T1 b) := forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u).

Hmm, this is the "R = SN + value-normal-form in V" definition, and V for arrows is defined in terms of R at subterms — well-founded by type structure? V(T1->T2) refers to R T1 and R T2 which are at smaller types. But R(T1->T2) refers to V(T1->T2) which refers to R T2 — that's structurally smaller, fine! And R(T1->T2) also needs "SN t" and multi-step closure. The definition is a plain Fixpoint on ty. 

Let me define:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (forall b, t -->* (tm_abs T1 b)?? ... )
```

Hmm, the issue: for a value v of type T1->T2, v must be an abs with domain T1. Then V(v) = forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u).

Let's write:

R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall b, t -->* (tm_abs T1 b) -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u).

Hmm, but should we require all intermediate reducts to be SN... SN t already says every reduct is SN. And every *normalizing* path ends at a normal form; but with SN we can do induction.

**Lemma 6 (main: fundamental theorem).** 

Statement: forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) -> R T (subst sigma t).

Where subst is a locally-nameless substitution on fvars. But wait, the language uses `open` for binders: T_Abs: for all x not in L, <{x |-> T1; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2}>. So the body of the abs is opened with a free variable. So define:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.
```

Hmm wait, but the standard issue: when opening under a binder, the indices shift. Let me define the general commutation lemma:

Consider t = tm_abs T t1 with the Rec under binder: open_rec j v (abs T t1) = abs T (open_rec (S j) v t1); subst σ of that = abs T (subst σ (open_rec (S j) v t1)) = abs T (open_rec (S j) (subst σ v) (subst σ t1)) by IH. Great, so the commutation lemma holds unconditionally (because substitution is "parallel" and doesn't shift indices; and σ applied to fvars gives arbitrary terms — but those terms are placed at the position where fvar was, and since fvars don't interact with de Bruijn indices, no capture issues arise in this identity). 

Wait, but there's a subtlety: in locally nameless, subst σ (bvar i) = bvar i, and (subst σ t) can contain bvars from σ's codomain. The identity holds syntactically. ✓ No lc needed here.

But here's where lc IS needed: the semantic environment condition uses R T (σ y) — if R doesn't include lc, then σ y could contain free bvars, and then... hmm, where would that break? Consider the T_Var case: R T (σ x) is given directly ✓. In the T_Abs case, the value u ∈ R T1 could be a term with free bvars. LHS = subst σ' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 (subst σ' (fvar x)) (subst σ' t1) = open_rec 0 u (subst σ' t1) ✓ = open (subst σ t1) u.

So we get R T2 (open (subst σ t1) u) directly for any u ∈ R T1. And that's what the value case of beta needs. But then the beta step is `app (abs T1 (subst σ t1)) u --> open (subst σ t1) u`, requiring `value u` and `locally_closed (abs T1 (subst σ t1))`. The value premise is given by the step constructor. So we need lc (abs T1 (subst σ t1)) only to know the step exists — but if we're *analyzing* a given step, the step's premises are available. However, if we're *proving* SN(app ...) by showing all reducts are SN, we need to handle each possible step; the premises come with the step.

But we also need SN(app (abs T1 body) u) to be derivable via SN_intro: forall t', app (abs T1 body) u --> t' -> SN t'. Given a step, cases:
- ST_AppAbs: premises lc(abs) and value u are available! So we need SN(open b u) = R T2 → SN ✓ (with b = body). Let's think concretely: lc_at k t means all free bvars i (with i >= k at depth-adjusted) — precisely, lc_at k t: bvars appearing must have index < k. Under a binder, the k increases. 

Claim: if lc_at k (open_rec j v t) and lc_at k v and j < k, then lc_at k t. Proof by induction on t:
- bvar i: open_rec j v (bvar i) = if i =? j then v else bvar i. If i = j: lc_at k (open_rec) = lc_at k v — but we need lc_at k (bvar i) = lc_at k (bvar j), requiring j < k ✓. If i ≠ j: lc_at k (bvar i) ✓ given.
- fvar ✓.
- app ✓.
- abs T t1: open_rec j v (abs T t1) = abs T (open_rec (S j) v t1), so we have lc_at k (abs) → lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S j) v t1). Need lc_at (S k) (abs T t1) i.e. lc_at (S k) t1. Apply IH with k := S k, j := S j: need S j < S k ✓ (j < k), lc_at (S k) v (from lc_at k v ✓ weakening). ✓.
- true/false/if: similar ✓.

Let me think much more carefully about the right logical relation for CBV.

**Standard CBV reducibility (from the literature, e.g. "Reducibility and PER" or Harper's PFPL chapter on CBV normalization?):**

For CBV, the standard approach uses **"closed" terms and a value-based logical relation with an "expansion" (anti-reduction) closure**:

Define the relation on terms as the smallest set closed under:
- if t is a value, t ∈ R(T) iff t ∈ V(T)
- if t --> t' (and t is not a value?), t ∈ R(T) if t' ∈ R(T)

I.e., R(T) = "expansion closure of V(T)". Formally:

Inductive R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall t, value t -> V T t -> R T t
| R_exp : forall t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t  -- expansion

Hmm, but this is an inductive definition with negative occurrence issues? No, it's a plain inductive family indexed by type, with V defined by recursion on T. This is the standard "reducibility candidates for CBV" (e.g., as in "A call-by-value lambda calculus with strong normalization" proofs, or in Girard's "Proofs and Types" adapted).

Wait, but if R is inductively defined by expansion, then every t ∈ R T has a chain t --> t1 --> ... --> v with v ∈ V(T). And SN follows if V(T) ⊆ SN and expansions preserve... hmm, we need: if all reduction paths from t end in V(T)-values, is t SN? Yes, provided the relation --> is such that... Actually the inductive definition R T t := closure under expansion from V(T) means there's a *specific* path t -->* v with v ∈ V(T). But SN requires ALL paths to terminate. Hmm, the inductive closure only guarantees one path. Hmm, but combined with... hmm.

Actually, the standard CBV reducibility candidate definition is:

R(T) := { t | SN t and for all v, t -->* v and value v implies v ∈ V(T) }.

This is the "SN + all value-reducts are in V" definition. Let me reconsider it with the annotation issue.

Actually, maybe better to define V semantically without the annotation issue by using "typed values". Hmm.

**Approach: prove SN via a semantic interpretation using the "value" relation only, with a Kripke-style / step-indexed definition:**

"t is in R T with SN-height ≤ n" — step-indexed by the SN derivation height. But that requires a notion of height for SN which isn't directly available (SN is inductive, but "height" requires classical reasoning or well-founded recursion).

**Alternative: use Coq's well-founded recursion over the SN relation.** Since SN t means the transitive closure of step from t is well-founded. We can define:

R T t := WF-ish... Hmm, actually, since strongly_normalizing t is defined inductively with exactly the "accessible" structure, we have `Acc step t` essentially. Indeed, SN t is the accessibility predicate for the relation step (with the direction: SN_intro : (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t — that's exactly Acc with the relation step). So `strongly_normalizing t` ≅ `Acc step t`. 

So we can do well-founded recursion on the SN derivation. This means we can define functions/predicates by recursion on SN derivations — but Coq's Fixpoint on an inductive Prop requires the elimination to be into Prop (fine!) with structural recursion. **Let me revisit the multi-step definition and handle the annotation issue by making the clause type-agnostic.**

Key realization: maybe we can define R's arrow clause as:

R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ lc t /\ forall b, t -->* tm_abs T1 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)

and prove the needed lemmas only for terms where the annotation matches. In the fundamental theorem, all terms come from typing, so annotations match. But the auxiliary "expansion" lemma needs care.

Let me try to structure the proof around this definition and see if the lemmas go through.

Setup:
```
Definition lc t := lc_at 0 t.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t /\ lc t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      SN t /\ lc t /\
      (forall b, t -->* tm_abs T1 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))
  end.
```

**Lemma R_SN**: forall T t, R T t -> SN t. (destruct T; simpl; tauto)
**Lemma R_lc**: forall T t, R T t -> lc t. (destruct T; simpl; tauto)

**Lemma SN_step**: SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'. (inversion on SN)
**Lemma SN_multi**: SN t -> t -->* t' -> SN t'.  (induction on multi; use SN_step)
**Lemma step_lc**: t --> t' -> lc t -> lc t'. 
**Lemma multi_lc**: t -->* t' -> lc t -> lc t'. 
**Lemma R_step**: R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
   induction on T (destruct T):
   - Bool: SN_step ✓; step_lc ✓.
   - Arrow T1 T2: SN_step ✓; step_lc ✓; clause: from t' -->* abs, get t -->* abs via multi_step with t --> t'; apply H's clause ✓.

Wait, careful: the hypothesis clause is quantified over b with `t -->* tm_abs T1 b`. Given t' -->* tm_abs T1 b, we build t -->* tm_abs T1 b ✓. Good. Note this doesn't even need the IH.

**R_multi**: R T t -> t -->* t' -> R T t'. By induction on multi using R_step.

Now, the fundamental theorem. But first, the crucial beta-expansion lemma. Let me think about what we need for T_Abs:

Goal: R (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 bs) where bs = subst σ t1, given the semantic typing of the body.

Components:
(a) SN(abs T1 bs) — trivial: `apply SN_intro; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep` — hmm, can a tm_abs step? Constructor inversion: ST_AppAbs's conclusion is tm_app ...; ST_App1, ST_App2 — all tm_app; ST_If* — tm_if. So no step from tm_abs ✓. Actually `inversion Hstep` should solve it.
(b) lc(abs T1 bs) — from has_type_lc + lc_at_subst. Or from the semantic proof: hmm. Let's use typing.
(c) forall b, abs T1 bs -->* tm_abs T1 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u).
    Given a multi-step from a value (abs) to abs T1 b: multi-step derivations from a value... The first step must be a step from abs T1 bs, which doesn't exist. So the multi-step must be multi_refl, hence b = bs. So the clause reduces to: forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open bs u) ✓ — which is exactly what we get from the IH + substitution lemma! 

So the multi-step definition makes (c) MUCH easier: multi-step from a value is just reflexivity (values can't step... wait, is that true? Can a value step? tm_true, tm_false: no rules conclude tm_true --> _. tm_abs: no rules conclude tm_abs --> _. So yes, values are normal forms ✓).

So we need:
**Lemma multi_value_inv**: value t -> t -->* t' -> t' = t. (By induction: the first step from a value is impossible.)

Then (c) is: for u ∈ R T1, R T2 (open bs u), which is what the T_Abs case gives via substitution.

So now, what else do we need?
- **T_App case**: t1 : T1->T2, t2 : T1. Need R T2 (app t1s t2s).
  Unfold R T2:
  - If T2 = Bool: need SN(app t1s t2s) and lc(app...).
  - If T2 = arrow T3 T4: need SN, lc, and forall b, app t1s t2s -->* abs T3 b -> forall u ∈ R T3, R T4 (open b u).
  
  So I need a general lemma: **R_app**: R (T1->T2) t1 -> R T1 t2 -> R T2 (app t1 t2).
  
  With the multi-step definition, the arrow clause of R (T1->T2) t1 says: forall b, t1 -->* abs T1 b -> forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (open b u).
  
  To prove R T2 (app t1 t2), we need to analyze the behavior of app t1 t2. Case analysis on t1's SN derivation + t2's SN derivation:
  
  We need SN(app t1 t2), lc, and the arrow clause.
  
  For the arrow clause: suppose app t1 t2 -->* abs T3 b. We need forall u ∈ R T3, R T4 (open b u). Hmm. How do we get this?
  
  Hmm, this is the crux again. Given a multi-step from app t1 t2 to an abstraction. Since t1 has type T1->T2 (well, semantically), and app t1 t2 reduces to an abstraction of type T3->T4 where T2 = T3->T4... The reduction from app t1 t2 must eventually do a beta where t1 reduces to an abstraction abs T1 b1 and t2 reduces to a value v. Then app t1 t2 -->* open b1 v -->* abs T3 b. So the result abstraction comes from reducing open b1 v.
  
  And from R arrow at t1: R T2 (open b1 v) (for v a value in R T1). And T2 = T3 -> T4, so R (T3->T4) (open b1 v) gives: forall b, open b1 v -->* abs T3 b -> forall u ∈ R T3, R T4 (open b u) ✓ — exactly what we need, since open b1 v -->* abs T3 b (as app t1 t2 -->* abs T3 b and the reduction is deterministic-ish...).
  
  So the proof requires: analyzing the multi-step reduction of app t1 t2, showing it factors through a beta at the "first" point, and that the resulting reduction chain open b1 v -->* abs T3 b. This is doable but requires careful reasoning about multi-step derivations and determinism of the relation.

  Note: the step relation is deterministic! (ST_AppAbs vs ST_App1 vs ST_App2 are mutually exclusive based on whether t1 is a value/abs and whether t2 is a value; for if, similar.) Proving determinism might be useful. But even with determinism, decomposing a multi-step from app t1 t2 requires induction on the multi derivation while tracking t1's and t2's reducts.

  Standard technique: prove "app t1 t2 reduces in a lock-step manner with t1 and t2". Specifically, we can prove a lemma:
  
  **Lemma multi_app_inv**: t1 -->* t1' -> t2 -->* t2' -> app t1 t2 -->* app t1' t2'... 
  
  For the arrow clause analysis: given app t1 t2 -->* abs T3 b, we want to find b1, v with t1 -->* abs T1 b1, t2 -->* v, value v, and open b1 v -->* abs T3 b.
  
  This "decomposition" lemma is standard in CBV normalization proofs: use induction on the multi-step derivation and case analysis. Alternatively, use a "strong" induction on SN to handle it.

Hmm, this is getting complicated. Let me consider an alternative formulation that avoids the "arrow clause over multi-steps" entirely, replacing it with a more "hereditary" formulation that's still well-founded.

**Alternative: define R using a Fixpoint over type with the clause "forall t', t --> t' -> R T t'" — the "step-closed" hereditarity — but well-founded by structural recursion via a *nested* well-foundedness?** 

Actually, here's a cleaner idea, the "**reducibility via SN-height / strong induction on SN**" restated as a **coinductive-free inductive family**:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_Bool : forall t, SN t -> lc t -> R Ty_Bool t
| R_Arrow : forall T1 T2 t,
    SN t -> lc t ->
    (forall t', t --> t' -> R (T1->T2) t') ->   (* step closure *)
    (forall u, value u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)) ->   (* application to values *)
    R (T1->T2) t.
```

This is an inductive family (no structural decrease on types needed since it's an inductive definition, not a Fixpoint)! The "negative occurrences" — R (T1->T2) t' appears in a premise — that's fine for inductive definitions (strict positivity: R occurs only positively? In `(forall t', t --> t' -> R (T1->T2) t')`, R occurs in the conclusion of an arrow, which is a positive position ✓. In `(forall u, value u -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u))`, R occurs in the domain of an arrow — that's a *negative* occurrence! Strict positivity would reject: `R T1 u -> ...` has R in a negative position. Hmm. Coq would reject "Non strictly positive occurrence of R".

But wait — we can work around it: define R as a Fixpoint on the type with the arrow case referring to R at *smaller* types (T1, T2), which is allowed. The problematic recursive reference is R at the SAME type (T1->T2) in the step-closure clause. In a Fixpoint, that's not structurally decreasing, so rejected.

Hmm, but we can split: define V (value interpretation) by Fixpoint on type (allowed, since V(T1->T2) refers to R T1 and R T2 at smaller types), and R as an inductive family generated by value + expansion:

```
Inductive R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall v, value v -> V T v -> R T v
| R_exp : forall t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t.
```
where V T v is defined by Fixpoint on T:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
  | Arrow T1 T2 => exists b, v = tm_abs T1 b /\ lc(v) /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)
  end.
```
But then V refers to R at smaller types (fine structurally), but R's inductive definition refers to V T which refers to R at smaller types — mutual but structurally decreasing in T. However, Coq's guard checker for mutual Fixpoint+Inductive... Hmm, defining an Inductive that refers to a Fixpoint which refers back to the Inductive — that's a mutual definition; Coq allows "Fixpoint ... with Inductive ..." (mutual fixpoint/inductive definitions) with the guard condition? Coq does support mutually recursive inductive and fixpoint definitions ("Scheme"? no). Actually, Coq supports `Fixpoint f ... with Inductive I ...` — yes, mutual fixpoint-inductive definitions are allowed in Coq (e.g., used for "well-founded trees" examples?). Hmm, I'm not 100% sure. There's `Inductive` + `Fixpoint` nesting via `with` — I believe Coq requires them to be in the same mutual block and checks structural recursion on the Fixpoint (with the inductive type as a "smaller" argument). Hmm, risky.

Alternative: make V not depend on R at all types but inline it. Let's think about which references are needed:
- V(Arrow T1 T2) v refers to R T1 and R T2 → smaller types.
- R T at all types is an inductive family with step closure.
- R T refers to V T.

Mutual:
```
Inductive R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall v, V T v -> R T v   (* with value v built into V *)
| R_exp : forall t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t
with V ... 
```
The reference V T → T → R at smaller types. Structural decrease on T in both directions. Coq's guard condition should accept mutual Fixpoint with T decreasing. But we need R to be an Inductive and V a Fixpoint. Coq does allow mutual Inductive-Fixpoint definitions? Let me think... I recall Coq does NOT allow mixing Inductive and Fixpoint in a mutual block. Hmm, actually there's `Fixpoint f ... with Inductive I ...` — I'm not sure.

Alternative: Define everything as an Inductive family with an index for "is this a value" — the classic Girard "candidates" encoded as:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_bool : forall t, SN t -> lc t -> R Bool t
| R_abs : forall T1 T2 b u ... 
```

Hmm, let me think about the shape we need semantically. What if we define:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_Bool : forall t, SN t -> lc t -> R Ty_Bool t
| R_AbsIntro : forall T1 T2 b,
    SN (tm_abs T1 b) -> lc (tm_abs T1 b) ->
    (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)) ->
    R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (tm_abs T1 b)
| R_Neutral : forall T t, ... 
```

The problem: R must contain ALL well-typed terms of a type, including applications like `app (abs T1 b) u` (which are not values). Those come from expansions: if t --> t' and R T t' then R T t. But the expansion rule is recursive at the same type — as an inductive family this is fine (R T t' is a premise, positive occurrence ✓). The only negative occurrence problem is `(forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))` in R_AbsIntro where T1, T2 are smaller types. Since R is a single inductive family indexed by ty, the strict positivity checker only cares about the occurrence structure, not the indices. `(forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))` — R occurs in the domain of an arrow (negative position), so Coq will reject it with "non-strictly-positive occurrence".

Workaround: use a *parameterized* definition where R at smaller types is a parameter (Fixpoint over types with the "negative" recursive reference at smaller types is fine in a Fixpoint!). So the right structure is:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ lc t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u))
  end.
```
Here the recursive calls are at smaller types, so the Fixpoint is accepted (it's structural recursion on T — yes, `R T1` and `R T2` where T1, T2 are subterms of `Ty_Arrow T1 T2` ✓).

The only problem with this definition was proving the beta-expansion case: showing R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u) from R T2 (open b u) and R T1 u.

Hmm, but wait: with the app-clause definition, R (T1->T2) (abs T1 b) requires forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u). And this needs the expansion property at T2. So we need a general "expansion under application" lemma. Let me think about how to prove it in a general way.

**Key general lemma needed**: If t is in R T (hereditarily), and t is "applicable"... Let me think about the closure property:

**Claim (Expansion)**: For all T, if M --> M' (one step), R T M', and M is lc, SN M ... hmm no: given R T M' (so SN M') we want R T M. SN M requires all reducts of M to be SN, which is not implied (other reducts could diverge). But in our case, there's exactly one beta-reduct and the other reducts are handled by induction.

Let me formulate it as the standard "**R is closed under head-expansion with R-arguments**":

Lemma beta_expand: forall T1 T2 (b : tm) (u : tm),
  R T1 u -> lc (tm_abs T1 b) -> value u -> R T2 (open b u) -> R T2 (app (tm_abs T1 b) u).

and then generalize to non-value u via induction on SN u:

Lemma beta_expand_gen: forall T1 T2 b u,
  lc (tm_abs T1 b) -> R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u) -> R T2 (app (tm_abs T1 b) u).

Proof by induction on SN u (which we have from R T1 u). Hmm, but as noted, when u --> u', we need R T2 (open b u') — not available.

Hmm! Right, the hypothesis "R T2 (open b u)" needs to be "for all reducts v of u that are values, R T2 (open b v)" — because in the CBV evaluation, the argument gets evaluated to a value before beta.

So the correct general hypothesis for the expansion lemma:

Lemma beta_expand_gen: forall T1 T2 b u,
  lc (tm_abs T1 b) ->
  (forall v, u -->* v -> value v -> R T2 (open b v)) ->
  SN u ->  (* or R T1 u *)
  R T2 (app (tm_abs T1 b) u).

Hmm, but if u is not a value, app(abs, u) steps to app(abs, u'), and the hypothesis "forall v, u' -->* v -> value v -> R T2 (open b v)" follows from the u-version ✓ by transitivity. OK so now, let me reconsider the whole structure. We have two candidate definitions:
(A) `R(Bool) = SN /\ lc`, `R(arrow T1 T2) t = SN t /\ lc t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)`.
(B) `R(Bool) = SN /\ lc`, `R(arrow T1 T2) t = SN t /\ lc t /\ forall b, t -->* tm_abs T1 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)`.

With (A), the T_App case is trivial (apply the clause), but the T_Abs case requires the beta-expansion lemma (as sketched). With (B), the T_Abs case is trivial-ish (values don't multi-step), but the T_App case requires decomposing multi-steps.

I think (A) is better because the beta-expansion lemma, while requiring an induction, has a clean statement. Let me carefully verify all the pieces of (A).

**Definition A:**
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t /\ lc t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

The recursive calls occur inside the definition: `R T1 u` and `R T2 (tm_app t u)`. Structural recursion on T ✓ (T1 and T2 are direct subterms). But Coq might complain because the recursive call is under a binder/inside a forall — that's fine for Fixpoints (the guard condition is about the arguments). Should be OK. Let me write:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ lc t

Hmm, the recursive calls R T1 and R T2 — Coq needs to see T1 and T2 as subterms. In the match branch for `Ty_Arrow T1 T2`, the recursive occurrences `R T1` and `R T2` are applied to smaller arguments ✓. Should pass.

Let me reconsider. How about the following definition, which is the classic "hereditarily strongly normalizing" (a.k.a. "SC" / "strongly computable"):

R(Bool) t := SN t
R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u)

and the key beta-expansion lemma in the literature for CBV:

Lemma: if forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (open b u), then abs T1 b ∈ R (T1->T2).

The proof: need SN(abs) ✓ and forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u). For arbitrary u ∈ R T1, prove R T2 (app abs u) by induction on T2? Let's try:

- T2 = Bool: SN(app abs u). Induction on SN u as before ✓ (the beta reduct open b u ∈ R Bool ✓ from hypothesis; ST_App2 handled by IH).
- T2 = T3 -> T4: need SN(app abs u) ✓ (as above, but this requires the induction to be simultaneous — hmm) and forall w ∈ R T3, R T4 (app (app abs u) w).

For the latter: Hmm. Note app (app abs u) w. Think of it as (app abs u) applied to w. By the definition of R (T3->T4) for the term (app abs u), we need forall w, R T4 (app (app abs u) w). Can we get this by the same argument at type T3->T4, using the hypothesis "forall u ∈ R T1, R (T3->T4) (open b u)"? We need a "nested" induction: the induction on SN u gives R T2 (app abs u) as the IH... and the IH would give R (T3->T4) (app abs u') for reducts u', which includes the clause forall w. But at u itself, we need R T4 (app (app abs u) w) for all w. 

Approach: prove by a secondary induction on SN w:
  app (app abs u) w:
  - If w is a value: app abs u -->* ... hmm, u might be a value too. Then app (app abs u) w: the inner app abs u steps to open b u (if u value), then (open b u) w -->* ... and R T4 (app (open b u) w) from the hypothesis R (T3->T4) (open b u) ✓. But the outer term (app (app abs u) w) steps to... hmm, in CBV, the outer app first evaluates the function (app abs u), so the first step is app (app abs u) w --> app (app abs u') w or app (open b u) w. So SN-wise it's fine. But proving R T4 of it requires SN + lc + clause; the clause for T4 needs another level of application...

The pattern is clear: we need a general lemma about "the relation is closed under expansion of the function part in an application spine". This is getting complicated because the definition unfolds infinitely for higher-order types.

**Better approach: use the "multi-step/value-based" definition (B), where the arrow clause is about values reached by multi-step.** With (B), the R_beta lemma becomes: 

R_beta: lc(abs T1 b) -> (forall v, u -->* v -> value v -> R T2 (open b v)) -> R T1 u → R T2 (app abs u).

Proof by induction on SN u:
- SN(app abs u): reducts: open b u (value u case) ✓ hypothesis; app abs u' ✓ IH.
- lc ✓.
- If T2 = T3->T4: need forall c, app abs u -->* abs T3 c -> forall w ∈ R T3, R T4 (open c w).
  Given app abs u -->* abs T3 c. If u is a value: the first step must be ST_AppAbs → open b u -->* abs T3 c, and the hypothesis gives R (T3->T4) (open b u) → its clause → R T4 (open c w) ✓.
  If u is not a value: the first step must be ST_App2 → app abs u' -->* abs T3 c; then IH gives R T2 (app abs u') → its clause ✓.
  
  So with definition (B), the R_beta lemma works by a single induction on SN u, because the arrow clause is "step-closed" rather than "application-closed". 

So definition (B) makes expansion easy but application hard; definition (A) makes application easy but expansion hard. Classic.

Hmm, what about combining: define R's arrow clause as BOTH the step-closure and the application condition? 

R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> R (T1->T2) t') /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u))

But the first forall is a recursive reference at the same type → not a Fixpoint (structural recursion fails). Could encode as an inductive? Negative occurrences again (the app clause has R T1 in a negative position).

Hmm, but what if we use the multi-step formulation to encode the step-closure:

R(T1->T2) t := SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall b, t -->* tm_abs T1 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))

For the T_App case (definition B), we needed to decompose multi-steps of app t1 t2. Let's think about how hard that is. Actually, maybe we can avoid the decomposition using SN induction on both t1 and t2:

To prove R T2 (app t1 t2) from R arrow t1 and R T1 t2 with definition (B), we need:
(i) SN(app t1 t2) — nested induction on SN t1, SN t2 as discussed (with ST_AppAbs case using R arrow's clause at t1 = abs T1 b, requiring the annotation... wait, we need t1 -->* tm_abs T1 b to apply the clause; t1 = tm_abs T0 b, so we need T0 = T1).

Hmm. Let me look at how the literature proves SN for CBV STLC. 

I recall the standard approach for call-by-value is due to **Plotkin / "call-by-value reducibility"** and uses the notion of "**R is the least set containing values and closed under expansion**", i.e., the inductive definition:

Inductive R T : tm -> Prop :=
| val : value-related
| exp : t --> t' -> R T t' -> R T t

This handles expansion by construction. Then SN is proven by showing that R is contained in SN, which requires "if all reducts... " hmm, the inductive R only has ONE expansion path. But combined with the property that R is closed under *all* steps (a lemma), we get SN: if t ∈ R and t --> t', then t' ∈ R (proved by induction on the R derivation, using determinism-ish reasoning), and then the R derivation gives a well-founded measure (the height of the R derivation decreases? Hmm, if t --> t' and t ∈ R, then t' ∈ R with a *smaller* derivation? For the exp constructor: t --> t', R T t'. If t ∈ R by exp(t --> t'', R t''), and t --> t' — is t' ∈ R with a smaller derivation? We'd need determinism: t' = t''. Then R t' is the subderivation ✓. For the val constructor: values don't step ✓. So by determinism of the step relation, R implies SN.

But then the definition needs V(T) to be defined... AND the mutual recursion issue: R is defined inductively referring to R at smaller types through V. Coq's positivity: R T1 u negative occurrence in V... 

Hmm OK. Let me think about how to encode this in Coq in a way that's accepted and workable.

**Key structural idea:** Define a single inductive family indexed by ty:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall T v, value v -> V T v -> R T v
| R_exp : forall T t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t
```
with V a Fixpoint:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => lc v /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open (destruct_abs v) u))
  end.
```
The mutual recursion between the Fixpoint V and the Inductive R: V calls R at smaller types; R calls V at the same type. Coq does NOT support mutual Inductive-Fixpoint definitions directly... Actually, hmm, I recall Coq DOES support `Fixpoint ... with Inductive ...`? Let me think. There is "mutual fixpoint" (Fixpoint ... with ...) and "mutual inductive" (Inductive ... with ...). Mixing them, I believe, is not supported. 

But there's a workaround: make V also an Inductive (mutual inductives):

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall T v, value v -> V T v -> R T v
| R_exp : forall T t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t
with V : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| V_bool : forall v, value v -> V Ty_Bool v
| V_arrow : forall T1 T2 v b, v = tm_abs T1 b -> lc v -> (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)) -> V (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) v.
```
Here in V_arrow, `R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)` has R in a negative position → rejected by the positivity checker.

Hmm. Damn.

Standard workaround: use **continuation/impredicative encoding** or the "**the negative occurrence is fine if the type indices decrease**" — but Coq's positivity checker doesn't track indices.

Let me reconsider the inductive definition approach with positivity workarounds.

**Inductive definition with positive-only occurrences:**

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_val : forall T v, value v -> V T v -> R T v
| R_exp : forall T t t', step t t' -> R T t' -> R T t
```

If V is a Fixpoint on T (not an Inductive), then this is an Inductive whose constructors refer to the Fixpoint V, which refers to R. Coq: can an Inductive refer to a previously-defined Fixpoint that refers to the Inductive? No — circular. Unless defined mutually.

Hmm, what if V doesn't refer to R, but instead R is defined for all types "uniformly" with the type as an index, using a single inductive family where the arrow case refers to R at arbitrary types (index-free positivity)? Like:

```
Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_bool : forall t, SN t -> lc t -> R Ty_Bool t
| R_abs : forall T1 T2 b, lc (tm_abs T1 b) -> (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)) -> R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (tm_abs T1 b)
| R_exp : ...
```
The negative occurrence `R T1 u -> ...` kills it.

**Workaround via a "closure" formulation:** Instead of `forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)`, use the multi-step/value characterization which has R in positive positions:

```
| R_abs : forall T1 T2 b, lc (tm_abs T1 b) ->
    (forall t', open b ??? )
```
Hmm, the beta condition fundamentally involves "forall u ∈ R T1, ...", which is negative.

**Trick: use a CPS/continuation encoding of the universal quantification.** In Coq, `forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u)` can be written using a universally quantified *predicate variable*? Impredicative encoding: define

`ArrowRel T1 T2 b := forall (P : tm -> Prop), (forall u, R T1 u -> P (open b u)) ...` no, same thing.

Alternative: define R *impredicatively* as a Fixpoint on the type where the arrow case is `forall (Q : tm -> Prop), (Q ⊆ R T2 ...)`. Hmm.

**Alternative: avoid the negative occurrence by stratifying the definition by type "rank" using a well-founded recursion on types**, i.e., definition (A). We're back to (A) and the expansion lemma.

OK so the general framework is fine. Let me now focus on the core difficulty: the beta-expansion lemma at higher types.

Let me look at the literature approach once more. I recall the standard CBV SN proof (e.g., in **"The Lambda Calculus: Its Syntax and Semantics"** or the **"call-by-value reducibility"** by **van Raamsdonk et al.**) uses the definition:

R(T) := { t | SN(t) } for base types
R(T1->T2) := { t | SN(t) and for all t', t -->* t' with t' a value, t' ∈ V(T1->T2) } — hmm.

And V(T1->T2) := { abs T1 b | forall u ∈ R(T1), open b u ∈ R(T2) }.

The key lemma is "**R is closed under expansion**": if t -->* v with v ∈ V(T), then t ∈ R(T). And "**R is closed under reduction**": if t ∈ R(T) and t --> t', then t' ∈ R(T). These are proven by induction on T.

Let's see: R(T) defined as "SN(t) and all value-reducts are in V(T)".

Expansion closure: t -->* v, v ∈ V(T) ⟹ t ∈ R(T)? That's not true for SN reasons alone: t might loop via another path. Hmm, but for deterministic systems, if t -->* v then all paths from t go to v or loop; SN isn't guaranteed. Hmm, in a deterministic system, if t -->* v (a normal form), then t is SN iff... all paths from t are finite, but a deterministic system has at most one path! So SN(t) ⟺ the path terminates. So for deterministic systems, t -->* v with v a normal form implies t is SN. YES — in a deterministic system, SN reduces to "the unique reduction path terminates", and "t -->* v with v a value (normal form)" implies t →* v and v is stuck/normal so the path terminates.

Wait, is our step relation deterministic? Let's check: for a term tm_app t1 t2:
- If t1 = abs T b: can ST_App1 apply? ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1', impossible for abs. ST_AppAbs applies if lc(abs) and value t2. ST_App2 applies if value t1 (abs is a value if lc) and t2 --> t2'. If t2 is a value, it can't step. So if t2 is a value: only ST_AppAbs ✓. If t2 is not a value: only ST_App2 (if lc abs) ✓. Deterministic ✓.
- If t1 is true/false (a value, not abs): ST_App1 no (no step), ST_AppAbs no (not abs), ST_App2: if t2 steps ✓ unique. 
- If t1 is not a value: ST_App1 applies if t1 --> t1' — which is unique if the whole relation is deterministic (induction). ST_AppAbs no, ST_App2 no (not value). ✓.
- Similarly for if and other cases.

But there's a subtlety: a term could be *stuck* (no steps) without being a value. For SN purposes, stuck terms are SN. So determinism means: each term has at most one reduct, hence SN(t) ⟺ the unique maximal reduction path is finite ⟺ t -->* v for some normal form v (stuck or value). 

So actually, with determinism, the proof becomes MUCH simpler: we can define R(T) := { t | t is SN } for base, and for arrows use the "value-reduct in V" formulation, where V(T1->T2)(abs T1 b) := forall u ∈ R(T1), open b u ∈ R(T2). And then show R(T) ⊆ SN by induction on T with the "all reduction paths terminate" argument. Hmm, but proving "t -->* v with v normal ⟹ SN t" requires reasoning about reduction paths, which with determinism requires a lemma: SN is equivalent to "no infinite path" and the "unique path" argument.

Hmm, we could actually prove something like: **SN t ↔ forall t', t -->* t' -> SN t'**? No...

Alternatively, define SN-equivalent notions: `SN t` is inductively defined; with determinism we can show: if t -->* v and v is a normal form (forall v', ~ v --> v') then SN t. Proof: induction on the multi-step derivation: 
- multi_refl: t = v is a normal form, so SN via SN_intro (no reducts) ✓.
- multi_step (t --> t' -->* v): by IH, SN t'; then SN t via SN_intro: for all t'', t --> t'' ⟹ t'' = t' (determinism!) ⟹ SN t'' ✓.
So we need **determinism**: t --> t1 -> t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.

That's a clean lemma to prove! With determinism, the whole proof might become much simpler, because SN can be established by showing "t reduces to a normal form".

So with determinism, we get the general anti-reduction closure:

**Lemma R_expand_one**: for all T t t', SN t -> t --> t' -> R T t' -> R T t.

Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: SN t needs all reducts SN; by determinism the only reduct is t' ✓ which is SN (from R T t'). lc t: from lc t' by "lc reflects steps" ✓.
- Arrow T1 T2:

  - lc ✓ (as above).
  - app clause: forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app t u). We have R T2 (app t' u) from R (T1->T2) t'. The step app t u --> app t' u exists (ST_App1 with lc u ✓). So with determinism + anti-reduction closure, the expansion lemma becomes easy:

To show R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u) from R T2 (open b u) [when u is a value]: apply R_expand_one' with the step app (abs T1 b) u --> open b u ✓ (requires lc(abs) and value u ✓).

And for non-value u: induct on SN u: app (abs T1 b) u --> app (abs T1 b) u' (ST_App2 with value(abs) ✓ — value requires lc abs ✓). Apply R_expand_one' with R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u') from the IH ✓. 

So the beta-expansion lemma holds with definition (A) + determinism! And the app clause at higher types? Let's re-examine: R (T1->T2) (abs T1 b) needs forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u). We prove it by induction on SN u with R_expand_one':
- If u is a value: R T2 (open b u) from the hypothesis (forall v ∈ R T1, R T2 (open b v)), and then R_expand_one' with the beta step ✓.
- If u is not a value: we'd like to show R T2 (app abs u) by induction on SN u. For each reduct u' of u, app abs u --> app abs u' (ST_App2, requires value (abs T1 b), i.e., lc(abs T1 b) ✓). Then R T2 (app abs u') by IH (u' ∈ R T1 forward closure, SN u' ✓). Then R_expand_one' gives R T2 (app abs u) ✓.

So yes! The generalization over "u is a value" isn't needed; the induction on SN u handles it, with R_expand_one' doing the work.

So the structure is:
1. R (definition A) — Fixpoint on T with SN, lc, and the app clause.
2. **Determinism of step** — needed for R_expand_one'.
3. **lc reflects steps** — for R_expand_one'.
4. R_expand_one' : t --> t' -> R T t' -> R T t.
5. R_beta_expand: forall T1 T2 b u, lc(abs T1 b) -> (forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (open b v)) -> R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 b) u).  [by induction on SN u, using R_expand_one' and forward closure]
6. Fundamental theorem with environments.
7. Final theorem.

Now, with determinism, let me double check R_expand_one':

**Lemma R_expand**: forall T t t', t --> t' -> R T t' -> R T t.
Proof by induction on T (destruct T):
- Bool: R Bool t = SN t /\ lc t. SN t: apply SN_intro; intros t'' Hstep; rewrite (step_det _ _ _ Hstep H) → SN t'' ✓ from R T t' (which is SN t'). lc t: from lc t' via step_lc_inv ✓.
- Arrow T1 T2: R = SN t /\ lc t /\ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app t u).
  - SN t ✓ (determinism).
  - lc t ✓ (step_lc_inv).
  - forall u ∈ R T1: need R T2 (app t u). We have R T2 (app t' u) from R (T1->T2) t'. The step: app t u --> app t' u via ST_App1 (premise lc u ✓ from R T1 u). Apply the IH at T2 (structurally smaller ✓): R T2 (app t u) ✓.

So the induction on T is structural (T1, T2 subterms). Coq's `induction T` gives IHs for T1 and T2 ✓.

Now, is the app clause of R at the arrow type consistent with *all* the terms we'll prove it for? Let's now re-examine the whole fundamental theorem with definition (A) + R_expand.

**Fundamental theorem**: 
```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, (forall x, lc (sigma x)) ->
    (forall x T', Gamma x = Some T' -> R T' (sigma x)) ->
    R T (subst sigma t).
```

Now let's also double check the T_Abs case with R_expand available:

**T_Abs case**: t = tm_abs T1 t1, T = T1 -> T2. Have IH: forall x, ~In x L -> forall σ, lc_env σ -> env_ok (update Γ x T1) σ -> R T2 (subst σ (open t1 (fvar x))).
Need: R (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst σ t1)).
- SN(abs) ✓ (values don't step → SN_intro with no reducts).
- lc(abs T1 (subst σ t1)): needs lc_at 1 (subst σ t1). From has_type_lc on the whole typing derivation (has_type Γ (abs T1 t1) (T1->T2)) → lc(abs T1 t1) → lc_at 1 t1. Then lc_at_subst with σ's terms lc_at 0 ✓ (using lc_env).
- app clause: forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst σ t1)) u).
  Let bs := subst σ t1.
  Step 1: Show Hbeta : forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (open bs v).
    Pick fresh x ∉ L ∪ fvs(t1). For v ∈ R T1, define σ' := update σ x v. Then:
    - lc_env σ': for y, if y = x then lc v ✓, else lc (σ y) ✓.
    - env_ok (update Γ x T1) σ': for y, T', update Γ x T1 y = Some T' → R T' (σ' y):
      - y = x: Some T' = Some T1 → T' = T1, σ' x = v ∈ R T1 ✓.

**Observation**: The step relation's reducts are determined by the term. For the app clause, the key insight is:

R T2 (app t u) can be proven if either:

**Lemma (R is closed under anti-reduction / beta)**: if V ∈ V(T1->T2) and U ∈ R(T1), then V U ∈ R(T2). This is the crucial one and it works nicely with (B'):

So the proof of beta for (B') requires: SN(V U) — nested induction on SN(V) and SN(U) — plus the value-reduct condition, which requires knowing that the multi-step value reducts of V U are either (a) from U's value reducts (handled by IH since (B')'s clause quantifies over all value reducts) or (b) from b[U/x] and its reducts (handled by R(T2)(b[U/x])'s clause with (B')).

Great. Now, the last piece: **is (B')'s definition well-founded as a Fixpoint?** 

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ lc t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      SN t /\ lc t /\
      (forall T0 b, t -->* tm_abs T0 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))
  end.
```

**Now the fundamental theorem details.**

Let me carefully define substitution and the environment notions.

```coq
Definition env_lc (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x, lc (sigma x).
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).
```

Fundamental theorem:
```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_lc sigma -> env_sem Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
```
Induction on the typing derivation. Hmm, but the T_Abs case's IH involves a forall over x. Let me check Coq's induction principle handling. The constructor:

```
| T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1,
    (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) ->
    has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)
```

`induction H` with motive `fun Gamma t T => forall sigma, env_lc sigma -> env_sem Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`:
- For T_Abs, the IH would be: forall x, ~In x L -> (forall sigma, env_lc sigma -> env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)))).
  This requires Coq to generalize the motive over Gamma, t, T and apply the IH to the premise. Coq's `induction H` should do this since the premise is an inductive occurrence under a binder; the induction principle gives exactly that IH. Hmm, I recall Coq's induction principle for constructors with premises that are functions: `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` — the IH is `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` ✓. Yes, Coq generates IHs for each recursive occurrence, including under binders ✓.

So the induction should give the right IH.

Let me write the cases:

**T_Var**: Gamma x = Some T, subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x. Apply env_sem ✓.

**T_True**: subst sigma tm_true = tm_true. R Ty_Bool tm_true = SN true /\ lc true.
- SN: SN_intro; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep (no rule concludes tm_true --> _) ✓.
- lc: lc_true ✓.

**T_False** ✓.

**T_App**: subst sigma (app t1 t2) = app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2). IH1: R (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1); IH2: R T1 (subst sigma t2). Apply R_app ✓ — with T2 = the codomain type. Note the typing constructor: `T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, <{Gamma |-- t1 \in T1 -> T2}> -> <{Gamma |-- t2 \in T1}> -> <{Gamma |-- app t1 t2 \in T2}>`. So the conclusion type is T2 ✓ and the function's type is Ty_Arrow T1 T2 ✓.

**T_If**: subst sigma (if t1 t2 t3) = if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3). IH1: R Bool t1s, IH2: R T t2s, IH3: R T t3s. Apply R_if ✓.

**T_Abs**: subst sigma (abs T1 t1) = abs T1 (subst sigma t1). Need R (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)):
- SN ✓ (no steps from abs).
- lc: need lc (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)), i.e., lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). From has_type_lc on the original typing derivation (has_type Gamma (abs T1 t1) (T1->T2)) → lc(abs T1 t1) → lc_at 1 t1. Then lc_at_subst with k := 1, using env_lc (forall x, lc (sigma x) = lc_at 0 (sigma x), weakened to lc_at 1 ✓ — hmm, my lc_at_subst statement requires `forall x, lc_at 0 (sigma x)`, and internally I'd need lc_at k (sigma x) for k = 1. Let me state lc_at_subst as: `lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t)` — with lc (sigma x) = lc_at 0, and internally weakening.
  Hmm, wait: is it true for all k? Let me double check with k = 1 and t = fvar x under a binder... lc_at 1 t means bvars < 1 (at the top level, i.e., bvar 0 is allowed). Substituting a bvar-free term for fvars: the result still has only bvar 0 (from t) ✓ < 1 ✓. Yes ✓.
- clause: forall T0 b, abs T1 bs -->* tm_abs T0 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u).
  By multi_value: b = bs and T0 = T1 (subst). Hmm, from `multi_value : value t -> t -->* t' -> t' = t` with value (abs T1 bs) ✓ (needs lc(abs T1 bs) ✓ which we just proved), we get tm_abs T0 b = tm_abs T1 bs → T0 = T1 and b = bs ✓.
  Then need forall u ∈ R T1, R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u) ✓ — via the Hbeta argument:
    Pick x fresh: x ∉ L and x ∉ fvs(t1).
    IH_x := IH x Hfresh : forall sigma', env_lc sigma' -> env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma' -> R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
    For u ∈ R T1: sigma' := update sigma x u.
    - env_lc sigma': for y, lc(update sigma x u y): destruct (Nat.eqb x y): if true, u — need lc u ✓ (R_lc); if false, sigma y ✓.
      Hmm, the definition `update Gamma x T = fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y`. So update sigma x u y = if eqb x y then u else sigma y.
    - env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma': for y, T', update Gamma x T1 y = Some T' -> R T' (update sigma x u y).
      Cases on eqb x y:
      + true: update Gamma x T1 y = Some T1, so Some T' = Some T1 → T' = T1 ✓ (injection). Then update sigma x u y = u ✓ ∈ R T1 ✓.
      + false: update Gamma x T1 y = Gamma y = Some T' → R T' (sigma y) ✓ (env_sem), and update sigma x u y = sigma y ✓.
    - So R T2 (subst (update sigma x u) (open t1 (fvar x))).
    - Rewrite using subst_open: subst σ' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 (subst σ' (fvar x)) (subst σ' t1) = open_rec 0 u (subst σ' t1). And subst σ' t1 = subst sigma t1 (by subst_update_fresh, x ∉ fvs(t1)) ✓ → open_rec 0 u (subst sigma t1) = open (subst sigma t1) u ✓.
    So R T2 (open bs u) ✓.

**Freshness**: need x ∉ L and x ∉ fvs(t1). Prove `exists x, ~ In x L /\ ~ In x (fvs t1)` — take a fresh atom for the concatenated list.

Now let me also double check: `env_sem` for `empty` in the final theorem: `forall x T, empty x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)` — since empty x = None, this is vacuous ✓ (`discriminate`).

Final theorem:
```coq
Theorem normalization : forall t T, has_type empty t T -> SN t.
Proof.
  intros t T H. 
  assert (R T (subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t)) by (apply fundamental; [intros x; simpl; apply lc_fvar | intros x T' Hc; discriminate]).
  rewrite subst_id in H0? 
```
Where `subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` ✓ by induction on t.

Then R_SN gives SN t ✓.

Now, let me reconsider the definition of R once more, and whether to include `lc` in the base case:

R Bool t = SN t /\ lc t
R (T1->T2) t = SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall T0 b, t -->* tm_abs T0 b -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open b u))

```coq
(* ---------- Substitution ---------- *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition ids : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_ids : forall t, subst ids t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```
Hmm, `congruence` should handle the recursive cases: `tm_app (subst ids t1) (subst ids t2) = tm_app t1 t2` with IHs ✓. Or use `f_equal; auto`. Let me use `induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; try (f_equal; auto)`. I'll test.

```coq
(* free variables *)
Fixpoint fvs (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => (x :: nil)%list
  | tm_app t1 t2 => (fvs t1 ++ fvs t2)%list
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvs t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => (fvs t1 ++ fvs t2 ++ fvs t3)%list
  end.

Fixpoint list_max (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (list_max xs)
  end.

Lemma list_max_upper : forall l x, In x l -> x <= list_max l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eassumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (list_max L)). intros Hin.
  apply list_max_upper in Hin. apply Nat.nlt_succ_diag_l in ... 
Qed.
```
`Nat.lt_succ_diag` hmm: we have x <= list_max L and x = S (list_max L), so S (list_max L) <= list_max L, contradiction with Nat.nle_succ_diag_l. Let me write: `pose proof (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l (list_max L))` : ~ S (list_max L) <= list_max L ✓. So:
```coq
Lemma fresh : forall L, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (list_max L)).
  intro Hin. apply list_max_upper in Hin.
  exact (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l (list_max L) Hin).
Qed.
```
✓

```coq
Lemma fresh_two : forall L1 L2, exists x, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2.
Proof.
  intros. destruct (fresh (L1 ++ L2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. split; intro Hin; apply Hx; apply in_or_app; [left | right]; assumption.
Qed.

  - simpl. rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.

Qed.

```coq
Lemma SN_multi : forall t t', SN t -> t -->* t' -> SN t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hm. induction Hm.
  - assumption.
  - apply IHHm. apply SN_step with (t := x); assumption.
Qed.
```
Hmm, multi's induction: multi_ind with constructors multi_refl (x) and multi_step (x y z, R x y -> multi R y z -> ...). The statement: forall t t', SN t -> multi step t t' -> SN t'. Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.

Qed.

Let me write a helper lemma:
```q
Lemma multi_value : forall t t', value t -> t -->* t' -> t' = t.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hv Hm. induction Hm.
  - reflexivity.
  - exfalso. apply (value_nostep x).  (* hmm *)
Qed.
```
where `value_nostep : forall t, value t -> forall t', ~ (t --> t')`. In the multi_step case: Hm : multi step x z with a step x y and multi y z; Hv : value x. We have a step x y and value x → contradiction ✓.

```q
Lemma value_nostep : forall t, value t -> forall t', ~ (t --> t').
Proof.
  intros t Hv t' Hst. induction Hv.
  - (* v_abs *) inversion Hst.
  - (* v_true *) inversion Hst.
  - (* v_false *) inversion Hst.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `induction Hv` might not be needed; `inversion Hv; subst; inversion Hst` hmm. Actually, inversion on the step gives conclusions of the form app/if, which contradict t being abs/true/false ✓. Now the substitution/environment machinery and the fundamental theorem.

```q
Definition env_lc (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x, locally_closed (sigma x).
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).
Definition env_update (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma env_update_eq : forall sigma x u, env_update sigma x u x = u.
Proof. intros. unfold env_update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma env_update_neq : forall sigma x u y, x <> y -> env_update sigma x u y = sigma y.
Proof. intros. unfold env_update. apply Nat.eqb_neq in H. rewrite H. reflexivity. Qed.
```

Hmm, `Nat.eqb_neq : forall x y, x <> y -> (x =? y) = false` ✓.

Also for contexts: `update Gamma x T1 y`: 
```q
Lemma update_eq : forall Gamma x T, update Gamma x T x = Some T.
Proof. intros. unfold update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.
Lemma update_neq : forall Gamma x T y, x <> y -> update Gamma x T y = Gamma y.
Proof. intros. unfold update. apply Nat.eqb_neq in H. rewrite H. reflexivity. Qed.
```
Note: `update` is defined as `fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y` ✓.

Now the fundamental theorem. Let me think about how to handle the T_Abs case in detail, particularly the generalization of the induction.

```q
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_lc sigma -> env_sem Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H; intros sigma Hlc Hsem.
```

T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1,
  (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) ->

  ...

So `induction H` should work if I state the goal appropriately. But careful: the goal is `R T (subst sigma t)` and `induction H` will generalize over Gamma, t, T, sigma? Hmm — sigma, Hlc, Hsem are in the context and depend on Gamma. Coq's `induction H` will revert sigma, Hlc, Hsem (since they depend on Gamma) and the goal ✓. Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)

```q
(** ============================================================ *)
(** Auxiliary development for the strong normalization proof.    *)
(** ============================================================ *)

(** ** Substitution for free variables *)

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition ids (x : atom) : tm := tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_ids : forall t, subst ids t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; congruence.
Qed.

Fixpoint fvs (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => (@nil atom)
  | tm_fvar x => (x :: nil)%list
  | tm_app t1 t2 => (fvs t1 ++ fvs t2)%list
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvs t1
  | tm_true => (@nil atom)
  | tm_false => (@nil atom)
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => (fvs t1 ++ fvs t2 ++ fvs t3)%list
  end.

Fixpoint list_max (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (list_max xs)
  end.

Lemma list_max_upper : forall l x, In x l -> x <= list_max l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eassumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (list_max L)).
  intro Hin. apply list_max_upper in Hin.
  exact (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l (list_max L) Hin).
Qed.

Lemma fresh_two : forall L1 L2 : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2.
Proof.
  intros L1 L2. destruct (fresh (L1 ++ L2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. split; intro Hin; apply Hx; apply in_or_app.
  - left; assumption.
  - right; assumption.
Qed.

Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma k v t,
  subst sigma (open_rec k v t) = open_rec k (subst sigma v) (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma k v t. revert k. induction t; intros k; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2, IHt3. reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Definition env_update (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma env_update_eq : forall sigma x u, env_update sigma x u x = u.
Proof. intros. unfold env_update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma env_update_neq : forall sigma x u y, x <> y -> env_update sigma x u y = sigma y.
Proof.
  intros. unfold env_update. apply Nat.eqb_neq in H. rewrite H. reflexivity.
Qed.

  ~ In x (fvs t) -> subst (env_update sigma x u) t = subst sigma t.

```q
Lemma SN_step : forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t HSN. induction HSN as [t Hred]. intros t' Hst. apply Hred. assumption.
Qed.

Lemma SN_multi : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t -->* t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.

  - assumption.
  - apply IHHm. apply SN_step with (t := x); assumption.
Qed.
```

Now the logical relation:

```q
Fixpoint RR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall (T0 : ty) (b : tm),
          t -->* tm_abs T0 b ->
          forall u : tm, RR T1 u -> RR T2 (open b u))
  end.

Lemma RR_SN : forall T t, RR T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof. intros T t H. destruct T; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.
```
Hmm, `destruct T` — for Ty_Arrow T1 T2, `simpl in H` unfolds RR ✓ H : SN t /\ (...) → tauto gives SN ✓. But careful: `simpl in H` when T is a variable doesn't reduce; after destruct ✓.

```q
Lemma RR_step : forall T t t', RR T t -> t --> t' -> RR T t'.
Proof.
  intros T. destruct T as [| T1 T2]; simpl; intros t t' HR Hst.
  - apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
  - destruct HR as [HSN Hclos]. split.
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
    + intros T0 b Hm u Hu. apply Hclos with (T0 := T0) (b := b).
      * apply multi_step with (y := t'); assumption.
      * assumption.
Qed.
```

```q

Lemma RR_app : forall (t1 : tm), strongly_normalizing t1 ->
  forall T1 T2, RR (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t1 ->
  forall t2, strongly_normalizing t2 -> RR T1 t2 -> RR T2 (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros t1 HSN1. induction HSN1 as [t1 Hred1 IH1].
  intros T1 T2 HR1 t2 HSN2. induction HSN2 as [t2 Hred2 IH2].
  intros HR2.
  simpl in HR1. destruct HR1 as [HSN1' Hclos1].
  assert (Hreds : forall s, tm_app t1 t2 --> s -> RR T2 s).
  { intros s Hst. inversion Hst; subst.
    - apply Hclos1 with (T0 := T) (b := t). apply multi_refl. assumption.
    - apply IH1 with (T1 := T1) (T2 := T2) (t2 := t2).
      + assumption.
      + apply RR_step with (t := t1); assumption.
      + apply RR_SN with (T := T1). assumption.
      + assumption.
    - apply IH2.
      + assumption.
      + apply RR_step with (t := t2); assumption. }

    + exact HSNapp.

      * (* refl: app = abs *) discriminate.
      * apply Hreds in Hst. simpl in Hst. destruct Hst as [Hx Hclosy].
        apply Hclosy with (T0 := T0) (b := c). assumption.
Qed.
```

Now the fundamental theorem:

```q
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> RR T (sigma x).

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> RR T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H; intros sigma Hsem.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Hsem. assumption.
  - (* T_Abs *) simpl.
    split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros s Hst. inversion Hst.
    + assert (Hbeta : forall v, RR T1 v -> RR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v)).
      { intros v Hv.
        destruct (fresh_two L (fvs t1)) as [x [HxL Hxf]].
        assert (Hsem' : env_sem (update Gamma x T1) (env_update sigma x v)).
        { intros y T' Hy. unfold update in Hy. unfold env_update.
          destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
          - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. inversion Hy. subst T'. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. exact Hv.
          - rewrite E. apply Hsem with T'. assumption. }
        specialize (IH x HxL (env_update sigma x v) Hsem').
        rewrite subst_open_rec in IH. simpl in IH.
        rewrite env_update_eq in IH.
        rewrite (subst_update_fresh sigma x v t1 Hxf) in IH.
        exact IH. }
      intros T0 b Hm u Hu.
      apply multi_abs with (T := T1) (b := subst sigma t1) in Hm.
      inversion Hm. subst.
      apply Hbeta. assumption.
  - (* T_App *) simpl. apply RR_app with (T1 := T1) (T2 := T2).
    + apply RR_SN with (T := Ty_Arrow T1 T2). apply IH1. assumption.
    + apply IH1. assumption.
    + apply RR_SN with (T := T1). apply IH2. assumption.
    + apply IH2. assumption.
  - (* T_True *) simpl. apply SN_intro. intros s Hst. inversion Hst.
  - (* T_False *) simpl. apply SN_intro. intros s Hst. inversion Hst.
  - (* T_If *) simpl. apply RR_if with (T := T).
    + apply RR_SN with (T := Ty_Bool). apply IH1. assumption.
    + apply IH1. assumption.
    + apply IH2. assumption.
    + apply IH3. assumption.
Qed.
```

Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->

Hmm wait, why does the standard identity fail here? Because `subst sigma` replaces fvars with arbitrary terms that may contain bvars that get captured by open_rec's substitution. R Bool t := SN t /\ lc t
